Well, I suppose the main thing is that I’ve noticed I just don’t seem to stay seated when other people do. It sounds small when I say it out loud, but it’s become a bit embarrassing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, very much so. I mean, looking back, it was one of those things that was sort of part of the family story, if that makes sense. My report cards were always, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the work,” but then there would be that “but” afterward—“but she doesn’t stay in her seat,” or “but she has trouble waiting her turn.” I remember seeing those comments more than once.

And my mother used to laugh about it, though I think she was also a bit worn out by it. She’d say I must have worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten, because I was constantly getting up for some reason. Sharpening a pencil, asking something, moving around—anything rather than just sitting there like the other children seemed able to do. At the time I don’t think I saw it as unusual, but yes, apparently other people definitely noticed.

My report cards were always, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the work,” but then there would be that “but” afterward—“but she doesn’t stay in her seat,” or “but she has trouble waiting her turn.” I remember seeing those comments more than once.\n\nAnd my mother used to laugh about it, though I think she was also a bit worn out by it. She’d say I must have worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten, because I was constantly getting up for some reason. Sharpening a pencil, asking something, moving around—anything rather than just sitting there like the other children seemed able to do. At the time I don’t think I saw it as unusual, but yes, apparently other people definitely noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main thing, no. I mean, I do sometimes lose the thread if something is very dry or if someone is going on for a long time, but I’ve always thought that was fairly normal, especially after a full day of teaching.

I mean, looking back, it was one of those things that was sort of part of the family story, if that makes sense. My report cards were always, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the work,” but then there would be that “but” afterward—“but she doesn’t stay in her seat,” or “but she has trouble waiting her turn.” I remember seeing those comments more than once.\n\nAnd my mother used to laugh about it, though I think she was also a bit worn out by it. She’d say I must have worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten, because I was constantly getting up for some reason. Sharpening a pencil, asking something, moving around—anything rather than just sitting there like the other children seemed able to do. At the time I don’t think I saw it as unusual, but yes, apparently other people definitely noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining your attention on tasks or conversations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main thing, no. I mean, I do sometimes lose the thread if something is very dry or if someone is going on for a long time, but I’ve always thought that was fairly normal, especially after a full day of teaching.\n\nWith tasks, if I know what I’m doing and there’s a clear purpose to it, I can usually keep with it. And in conversations, I don’t generally feel as though I’m drifting away or unable to follow, unless I’m already tired or the conversation is terribly slow. It’s more that I feel uncomfortable sitting still through the whole thing, if that makes sense. But in terms of attention itself—sustaining it—I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you’re sitting and then get up, does it usually feel like an inner physical restlessness that builds up until you move?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Leaving_Seat\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is that I keep getting up in meetings or at dinner when everyone else is seated, like I'll suddenly need to refill coffee, check a handout, or walk to the back of the room even though nothing is really urgent.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },

  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not usually that I have some very sensible reason to stand up, though at the time I dress it up as one. I’ll be in a staff meeting, for example, and suddenly I’ll think, “Oh, I should just refill my coffee,” or “I need to look at that handout at the back,” or I’ll get up to check something that really could have waited.

It’s more like sitting there starts to feel almost… uncomfortable, not painful, but like my body is insisting I do something. And then once I’m up, there’s a bit of relief, even if I realize afterward that there wasn’t actually anything urgent. The embarrassing part is that everyone else is still seated, perfectly normal, carrying on with the meeting or dinner, and I’m the one who has popped up again for no especially good reason.

No, I don’t think so. At least, that’s not how it feels to me.

It isn’t like I’m sitting there thinking, “If I don’t get up, something bad will happen,” or trying to push away a particular thought. It’s more physical than mental, if that makes sense. I might have thoughts attached to it afterward—like inventing a reason to get up, or telling myself I need to fetch something—but I wouldn’t say there’s a worry underneath it that I’m trying to quiet.

I suppose there are times when I’m stressed and I’m probably more restless, but the urge to move doesn’t seem to come from one specific fear or intrusive idea. It’s just this sort of internal pressure to be up and doing something.

My report cards were always, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the work,” but then there would be that “but” afterward—“but she doesn’t stay in her seat,” or “but she has trouble waiting her turn.” I remember seeing those comments more than once.\n\nAnd my mother used to laugh about it, though I think she was also a bit worn out by it. She’d say I must have worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten, because I was constantly getting up for some reason. Sharpening a pencil, asking something, moving around—anything rather than just sitting there like the other children seemed able to do. At the time I don’t think I saw it as unusual, but yes, apparently other people definitely noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining your attention on tasks or conversations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main thing, no. I mean, I do sometimes lose the thread if something is very dry or if someone is going on for a long time, but I’ve always thought that was fairly normal, especially after a full day of teaching.\n\nWith tasks, if I know what I’m doing and there’s a clear purpose to it, I can usually keep with it. And in conversations, I don’t generally feel as though I’m drifting away or unable to follow, unless I’m already tired or the conversation is terribly slow. It’s more that I feel uncomfortable sitting still through the whole thing, if that makes sense. But in terms of attention itself—sustaining it—I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: When you’re sitting and then get up, does it usually feel like an inner physical restlessness that builds up until you move?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not usually that I have some very sensible reason to stand up, though at the time I dress it up as one. I’ll be in a staff meeting, for example, and suddenly I’ll think, “Oh, I should just refill my coffee,” or “I need to look at that handout at the back,” or I’ll get up to check something that really could have waited.\n\nIt’s more like sitting there starts to feel almost… uncomfortable, not painful, but like my body is insisting I do something. And then once I’m up, there’s a bit of relief, even if I realize afterward that there wasn’t actually anything urgent. The embarrassing part is that everyone else is still seated, perfectly normal, carrying on with the meeting or dinner, and I’m the one who has popped up again for no especially good reason.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to get up, is it because you’re trying to quiet a particular worry or intrusive thought?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, that’s not how it feels to me.\n\nIt isn’t like I’m sitting there thinking, “If I don’t get up, something bad will happen,” or trying to push away a particular thought. It’s more physical than mental, if that makes sense. I might have thoughts attached to it afterward—like inventing a reason to get up, or telling myself I need to fetch something—but I wouldn’t say there’s a worry underneath it that I’m trying to quiet.\n\nI suppose there are times when I’m stressed and I’m probably more restless, but the urge to move doesn’t seem to come from one specific fear or intrusive idea. It’s just this sort of internal pressure to be up and doing something.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve certainly had good moods and bad moods like anyone else, and I can be a bit sharp if I’m tired or if the day has been especially trying, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a period where I felt unusually euphoric or not myself in that way.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.1, F31.9, F95.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the main thing is that I’ve noticed I just don’t seem to stay seated when other people do. It sounds small when I say it out loud, but it’s become a bit embarrassing. In meetings at school, or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up—like I’ll decide I need to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room for some reason. And often, when I think about it afterward, there really wasn’t anything urgent at all. Everyone else is just sitting and carrying on, and I’m the one popping up and moving around. That’s what made me think I should come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you often described as fidgety or unable to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. I mean, looking back, it was one of those things that was sort of part of the family story, if that makes sense. My report cards were always, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the work,” but then there would be that “but” afterward—“but she doesn’t stay in her seat,” or “but she has trouble waiting her turn.” I remember seeing those comments more than once.\n\nAnd my mother used to laugh about it, though I think she was also a bit worn out by it. She’d say I must have worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten, because I was constantly getting up for some reason. Sharpening a pencil, asking something, moving around—anything rather than just sitting there like the other children seemed able to do. At the time I don’t think I saw it as unusual, but yes, apparently other people definitely noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining your attention on tasks or conversations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main thing, no. I mean, I do sometimes lose the thread if something is very dry or if someone is going on for a long time, but I’ve always thought that was fairly normal, especially after a full day of teaching.\n\nWith tasks, if I know what I’m doing and there’s a clear purpose to it, I can usually keep with it. And in conversations, I don’t generally feel as though I’m drifting away or unable to follow, unless I’m already tired or the conversation is terribly slow. It’s more that I feel uncomfortable sitting still through the whole thing, if that makes sense. But in terms of attention itself—sustaining it—I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: When you’re sitting and then get up, does it usually feel like an inner physical restlessness that builds up until you move?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not usually that I have some very sensible reason to stand up, though at the time I dress it up as one. I’ll be in a staff meeting, for example, and suddenly I’ll think, “Oh, I should just refill my coffee,” or “I need to look at that handout at the back,” or I’ll get up to check something that really could have waited.\n\nIt’s more like sitting there starts to feel almost… uncomfortable, not painful, but like my body is insisting I do something. And then once I’m up, there’s a bit of relief, even if I realize afterward that there wasn’t actually anything urgent. The embarrassing part is that everyone else is still seated, perfectly normal, carrying on with the meeting or dinner, and I’m the one who has popped up again for no especially good reason.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to get up, is it because you’re trying to quiet a particular worry or intrusive thought?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, that’s not how it feels to me.\n\nIt isn’t like I’m sitting there thinking, “If I don’t get up, something bad will happen,” or trying to push away a particular thought. It’s more physical than mental, if that makes sense. I might have thoughts attached to it afterward—like inventing a reason to get up, or telling myself I need to fetch something—but I wouldn’t say there’s a worry underneath it that I’m trying to quiet.\n\nI suppose there are times when I’m stressed and I’m probably more restless, but the urge to move doesn’t seem to come from one specific fear or intrusive idea. It’s just this sort of internal pressure to be up and doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when your mood was unusually high, euphoric, or unusually irritable for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve certainly had good moods and bad moods like anyone else, and I can be a bit sharp if I’m tired or if the day has been especially trying, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a period where I felt unusually euphoric or not myself in that way.\n\nAnd with irritability… I suppose teaching can make anyone irritable at times, especially after years of it, but it feels understandable to the situation rather than some separate change in my mood. I haven’t noticed anything where people around me would say, “You’re acting completely different,” or where I felt suddenly high or unusually driven by my mood. So, no—not that I’m aware of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t think so, no. Not in the way you’re describing, at least.

I mean, I might clear my throat if I’ve been talking all day, which I do quite a lot as a teacher, or blink more if my eyes are tired, but I wouldn’t say I have a repeated movement or sound that just happens on its own over and over. I’ve never had anyone point out that I’m shrugging or twitching or making noises, and it’s not something I’ve noticed in myself.

So, no—nothing I’d recognize as tics, unless there’s something more subtle you mean.

I’ll be in a staff meeting, for example, and suddenly I’ll think, “Oh, I should just refill my coffee,” or “I need to look at that handout at the back,” or I’ll get up to check something that really could have waited.\n\nIt’s more like sitting there starts to feel almost… uncomfortable, not painful, but like my body is insisting I do something. And then once I’m up, there’s a bit of relief, even if I realize afterward that there wasn’t actually anything urgent. The embarrassing part is that everyone else is still seated, perfectly normal, carrying on with the meeting or dinner, and I’m the one who has popped up again for no especially good reason.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to get up, is it because you’re trying to quiet a particular worry or intrusive thought?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, that’s not how it feels to me.\n\nIt isn’t like I’m sitting there thinking, “If I don’t get up, something bad will happen,” or trying to push away a particular thought. It’s more physical than mental, if that makes sense. I might have thoughts attached to it afterward—like inventing a reason to get up, or telling myself I need to fetch something—but I wouldn’t say there’s a worry underneath it that I’m trying to quiet.\n\nI suppose there are times when I’m stressed and I’m probably more restless, but the urge to move doesn’t seem to come from one specific fear or intrusive idea. It’s just this sort of internal pressure to be up and doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when your mood was unusually high, euphoric, or unusually irritable for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve certainly had good moods and bad moods like anyone else, and I can be a bit sharp if I’m tired or if the day has been especially trying, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a period where I felt unusually euphoric or not myself in that way.\n\nAnd with irritability… I suppose teaching can make anyone irritable at times, especially after years of it, but it feels understandable to the situation rather than some separate change in my mood. I haven’t noticed anything where people around me would say, “You’re acting completely different,” or where I felt suddenly high or unusually driven by my mood. So, no—not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden movements or sounds that happen over and over, like blinking, shrugging, throat-clearing, or other tics?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the way you’re describing, at least.\n\nI mean, I might clear my throat if I’ve been talking all day, which I do quite a lot as a teacher, or blink more if my eyes are tired, but I wouldn’t say I have a repeated movement or sound that just happens on its own over and over. I’ve never had anyone point out that I’m shrugging or twitching or making noises, and it’s not something I’ve noticed in myself.\n\nSo, no—nothing I’d recognize as tics, unless there’s something more subtle you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not sure I’d say excessively, no. I’ll be in a staff meeting, for example, and suddenly I’ll think, “Oh, I should just refill my coffee,” or “I need to look at that handout at the back,” or I’ll get up to check something that really could have waited.\n\nIt’s more like sitting there starts to feel almost… uncomfortable, not painful, but like my body is insisting I do something. And then once I’m up, there’s a bit of relief, even if I realize afterward that there wasn’t actually anything urgent. The embarrassing part is that everyone else is still seated, perfectly normal, carrying on with the meeting or dinner, and I’m the one who has popped up again for no especially good reason.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to get up, is it because you’re trying to quiet a particular worry or intrusive thought?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, that’s not how it feels to me.\n\nIt isn’t like I’m sitting there thinking, “If I don’t get up, something bad will happen,” or trying to push away a particular thought. It’s more physical than mental, if that makes sense. I might have thoughts attached to it afterward—like inventing a reason to get up, or telling myself I need to fetch something—but I wouldn’t say there’s a worry underneath it that I’m trying to quiet.\n\nI suppose there are times when I’m stressed and I’m probably more restless, but the urge to move doesn’t seem to come from one specific fear or intrusive idea. It’s just this sort of internal pressure to be up and doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when your mood was unusually high, euphoric, or unusually irritable for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve certainly had good moods and bad moods like anyone else, and I can be a bit sharp if I’m tired or if the day has been especially trying, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a period where I felt unusually euphoric or not myself in that way.\n\nAnd with irritability… I suppose teaching can make anyone irritable at times, especially after years of it, but it feels understandable to the situation rather than some separate change in my mood. I haven’t noticed anything where people around me would say, “You’re acting completely different,” or where I felt suddenly high or unusually driven by my mood. So, no—not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden movements or sounds that happen over and over, like blinking, shrugging, throat-clearing, or other tics?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the way you’re describing, at least.\n\nI mean, I might clear my throat if I’ve been talking all day, which I do quite a lot as a teacher, or blink more if my eyes are tired, but I wouldn’t say I have a repeated movement or sound that just happens on its own over and over. I’ve never had anyone point out that I’m shrugging or twitching or making noises, and it’s not something I’ve noticed in myself.\n\nSo, no—nothing I’d recognize as tics, unless there’s something more subtle you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying excessively about many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say excessively, no. I mean, I do worry about ordinary things—work, whether I’ve handled something properly with a student, family bits and pieces, practical things that need doing—but I don’t feel as though I’m constantly worrying across lots of areas of my life in a way that takes over.\n\nSometimes I’ll go over something in my head more than I need to, especially if it’s been a busy day, but it doesn’t feel like this broad, uncontrollable worrying about everything. It’s more just… normal concerns, I think. Unless I’m underestimating it, but I wouldn’t describe myself as a chronic worrier."
  }
]

Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness. Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. She’d say I must have worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten, because I was constantly getting up for some reason. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
